## Page 1


UNIVERSAL 2-PARAMETER N = 2 SUPERSYMMETRIC W∞-ALGEBRA
THOMAS CREUTZIG, VOLODYMYR KOVALCHUK, ANDREW R. LINSHAW, ARIM SONG, AND UHI RINN SUH
ABSTRACT. The universal 2-parameter vertex algebra W∞of type W(2, 3, . . . ) is a classifying object for
vertex algebras of type W(2, 3, . . . , N) for some N; under mild hypotheses, all such vertex algebras arise
as quotients of W∞. In 2017, Gaiotto and Rapˇc´ak introduced a family of such vertex algebras called Y -
algebras, and conjectured that they fall into groups of three that are mutually isomorphic. This is a common
generalization of both Feigin-Frenkel duality and the coset realization of principal W-algebras in type A, and
was proven in 2021 for the simple Y -algebras (i.e., one label is zero) by the first and third authors. In this
paper, we extend this entire story to the N = 2 superconformal setting. First, we prove the 2013 conjecture
of Gaberdiel and Candu that there exists a universal 2-parameter vertex algebra WN =2
∞
which is an extension
of the N = 2 superconformal algebra, and has four additional generators in weights i, i+ 1
2, i+ 1
2, i+1, for each
integer i > 1. This admits many 1-parameter quotients which we call N = 2 supersymmetric Y -algebras,
and we prove the dualities among these algebras which were conjectured in 2018 by Prochazka and Rapˇc´ak.
A special case is the coset realization of the principal W-algebra Wk(sln+1|n) which was conjectured in 1992
by Ito. As a corollary, we obtain the strong rationality of Wk(sln+1|n) for k = −1 +
1
n+a+1 for all positive
integers n, a, and we describe its module category.
This generalizes Adamovi´c’s 1999 result on N = 2
minimal models, which is the case n = 1.
1. Introduction
W-algebras are a class of vertex algebras that are a common generalization of affine vertex algebras and
the Virasoro algebra. Given a Lie (super)algebra g and a nilpotent element F in the even part of g, the
W-algebra Wk(g, F) at level k ∈C was defined by Kac, Roan and Wakimoto in [72], generalizing earlier
constructions of Feigin and Frenkel [55]. The best studied case is when F is a principal nilpotent; Wk(g, F)
is then called a principal W-algebra and is denoted by Wk(g). Principal W-algebras satisfy Feigin-Frenkel
duality, which is a vertex algebra isomorphism Wk(g) ∼= Wk′(Lg). Here Lg is the Langlands dual Lie algebra,
h∨, Lh∨are the dual Coxeter numbers of g, Lg, and (k+h∨)(k′ +L h∨) = r where r is the lacing number [54].
For g simply-laced, there is another duality called the coset realization which was proven [16]. For generic
values of ℓ, we have a vertex algebra isomorphism
(1.1)
Wℓ(g) ∼= Com(V k+1(g), V k(g) ⊗L1(g)),
ℓ+ h∨=
k + h∨
(k + 1) + h∨,
which descends to an isomorphism of simple vertex algebras Wℓ(g) ∼= Com(Lk+1(g), Lk(g) ⊗L1(g)) for all
admissible levels k. This was a longstanding conjecture [19, 53, 57, 73], generalizing the Goddard-Kent-Olive
(GKO) construction of the Virasoro algebra [65]. A shorter proof had been found later [46]. When k is an
admissible level for bg, ℓis a nondegenerate admissible level for bg, so that Wℓ(g) is strongly rational (i.e., lisse
and rational) by [9, 10]. There is also a coset realization for type B appearing in [41]:
(1.2)
Wℓ(so2n+1) ∼= Com(V k(sp2n), V k(osp1|2n)),
ℓ+ h∨
so2n+1 =
k + h∨

Non-principal W-algebras as well as W-superalgebras have become important in physics in recent years;
see for example [12, 29, 31, 32, 58, 59, 89, 90, 91]. In [58], Gaiotto and Rapˇc´ak introduced a family of 1-
parameter vertex algebras YN1,N2,N3[ψ] called Y -algebras, which are indexed by three integers N1, N2, N3 ≥0
and ψ ∈C. They are associated to interfaces of twisted N = 4 supersymmetric gauge theories with gauge
groups U(N1), U(N2), and U(N3). The interfaces satisfy a permutation symmetry which led Gaiotto and
Rapˇc´ak to conjecture a triality of isomorphisms of Y -algebras. The Y -algebras with one label zero are (up
to a Heisenberg algebra) the affine cosets of a family of non-principal W-(super)algebras of type A which
are known as hook-type. For n ≥1 and m ≥0, define
Wψ(n, m) := Wk(sln+m, Fn,1m),
where Fn,1m corresponds to the hook-type partition (n, 1, 1, . . . , 1) of n + m, and ψ = k + n + m. It has
affine subalgebra V ψ−m−1(glm), and we define
(1.3)
Cψ(n, m) := Com(V ψ−m−1(glm), Wψ(n, m)).
When n = 0, we instead define
(1.4)
Cψ(0, m) := Com(V k−1(glm), V k(slm) ⊗S(m)).
Here S(m) is the rank m βγ-system, which has an action of L−1(glm).
Similarly, for n ≥1, m ≥0, and m ̸= n, we define
Vψ(n, m) := Wk(sln|m, Fn|1m),
where Fn|1m is principal in the subalgebra sln and trivial in slm, and ψ = k +n−m. It has affine subalgebra
V −ψ−m+1(glm), and we define
(1.5)
Dψ(n, m) := Com(V −ψ−m+1(glm), Vψ(n, m)).
For m = n ≥2, we define Dψ(n, n) := Com(V −ψ−n+1(sln), Vψ(n, n))GL1, where Vψ(n, n) := Wk(psln|n, Fn|1n).
When n = 0, we set
(1.6)
Dψ(0, m) := Com(V −k+1(glm), V −k(slm) ⊗E(m)).
Here E(m) is the rank m bc-system, which has an action of L1(glm). Note that (1.6) is isomorphic to (1.1)
for g = slm, so we also regard (1.6) as a GKO coset. Finally, we define Dψ(1, 1) := A(1)GL1 where A(1) is
the rank 1 symplectic fermion algebra.
Theorem 1.1. [40] For integers n ≥m ≥0, we have isomorphisms of 1-parameter vertex algebras

Dψ(n, m) ∼= Cψ−1(n −m, m) ∼= Dψ′(m, n),

The cases Dψ(n, 0) ∼= Cψ−1(n, 0) and Dψ(n, 0) ∼= Dψ′(0, n) are just Feigin-Frenkel duality and the coset
realization, respectively. The key idea in the proof of (1.7) that Cψ(n, m) and Dψ(n, m) can be realized ex-
plicitly as simple 1-parameter quotients of the universal 2-parameter vertex algebra W∞of type W(2, 3, . . . ).
This is a classifying object for vertex algebras of type W(2, 3, . . . , N) for some N satisfying some mild hy-
potheses. It was known to physicists since the early 1990s, and was constructed rigorously by the third
author in [81].

[39, Theorem 8.1], Ito’s conjecture would imply the isomorphism of simple quotients Wk(sln+1|n) ∼= Cℓ(n)
when ℓis admissible. It is apparent that Cℓ(n) is unitary when ℓis a positive integer, and it was shown to
be strongly rational in [16]. Therefore the isomorphism would imply the strong rationality of Wk(sln+1|n)
at these levels. This is a natural generalization of the strong rationality of the N = 2 minimal models [1].
1.2. SUSY W-algebras. An important insight into the structure of Wk(sln+1|n) was obtained from the
concept of a supersymmetric (SUSY) W-algebra, first introduced by Madsen and Ragoucy [83] and developed
mathematically by Molev, Ragoucy and the fifth author in [87].
Given a basic Lie superalgebra g and
an odd nilpotent element contained in an osp1|2-subalgebra of g, the SUSY W-algebra is constructed via
the supersymmetric analogue of the BRST complex. By its construction, any SUSY W-algebra naturally
has an N = 1 supersymmetry and, moreover, it is a conformal extension of the N = 1 superconformal
algebra Virc
N=1.
Recently, the fourth and fifth authors and Genra in [64] showed that the SUSY W-
algebra Wk
N=1(g) associated with the odd principal nilpotent f is isomorphic to the principal W-algebra
Wk(g). This implies the intrinsic SUSY structure of Wk(g). In particular, when g = sln+1|n and fn+1|n
is its odd principal nilpotent, Fn+1|n = 1
2[fn+1|n, fn+1|n] is an even principal nilpotent and Wk(sln+1|n) ∼=
Wk
N=1(sln+1|n, fn+1|n). The N = 2 supersymmetry of Wk(sln+1|n), understood as a conformal extension of
Virc
N=2 with the remaining generators arranged into N = 2 diamonds, essentially follows from the existence
of two independent odd nilpotents fn+1|n and ˜fn+1|n satisfying [fn+1|n, fn+1|n] = [ ˜fn+1|n, ˜fn+1|n] and hence
(1.12)
Wk
N=1(sln+1|n, fn+1|n) ∼= Wk(sln+1|n) ∼= Wk
N =1(sln+1|n, ˜fn+1|n).
The details will be explained in Section 5. In addition, all the strong generators of Wk
N =1(sln+1|n) can be
obtained by a column determinant formula of a matrix [87]. Using the SUSY Miura map on Wk
N =1(sln+1|n)
which induces the free field realization [96], one can find OPE relations between generators.

Our first main result, which is a paraphrasing of Theorems 7.15, 7.20, and 7.16, is the following
Theorem 1.2. There exists a unique 2-parameter vertex algebra WN =2
∞
with the following features:
(1) It is defined over the localization of the polynomial ring C[c, λ] obtained by inverting c, c −1, and
c + 3.
(2) It is freely generated of type (1.13), and weakly generated by the fields in weights 1, 3
2, 2.
(3) Fields H, G±, L of weights 1, 3
2, 2 generate a copy of Virc
N =2.
(4) For each i ≥2, we have fields W i,⊥, W i,±, W i,⊤of conformal weights i, i + 1
2, i + 1
2, i + 1, which
generate an N = 2 diamond.
Moreover, WN=2
∞
serves as a classifying object for vertex algebras with these properties; any vertex algebra
with a strong generating set of type (1.13) (not necessarily minimal) satisfying the above conditions, is a
quotient of WN=2
∞
.
The construction is similar to the construction of W∞given in [76]; WN =2

is defined as the universal
enveloping vertex algebra of a nonlinear Lie conformal superalgebra [49]. In [22], it was also conjectured
that for i ≥2, the fields {W i,⊥, W i,±, W i,⊤} could be assumed primary for Virc
N =2, and we will prove this
in Appendix A; see Corollary A.5.

∞

Moreover, Theorem 10.5 gives a complete list of inequivalent simple modules, including Ramond twisted
modules, as well as their fusion rules.
This generalizes the strong rationality of the N = 2 minimal models VirN =2,c for c = 3(a−1)
a+1 , which was
first proved by Adamovi´c in [1]. The classification of simple modules as well as the computation of fusion
rules is done via a version of level-rank duality and this requires us to extend the mirror equivalence theorem
for a pair of rational vertex algebras U, V [77, 85], to the case when U, V can be vertex superalgebras; see
Section 10.1 for this generalization.
In Appendices B and C we extend some results of the first and third authors on cosets of affine vertex
algebras V k(g) inside larger vertex algebras [39] for reductive Lie algebras g, to the case when g is a basic Lie
superalgebra or gln|n. These results are needed in our description of Cψ
N =2(n, r|s), and are of independent
interest.

## Page 6


1.4. Applications. Very much like the W∞-algebra and its even spin cousin have many consequences for
the structure and representation theory of W-algebras, we expect to also find a wide variety of applications
of our construction of WN=2
∞
. For example, using a convolution operation the dualities of Feigin-Frenkel
type between Y -algebras were upgraded to a functor between the representation categories of the W-algebras
whose cosets are isomorphic [43]. A similar result should work as well in the N = 2 supersymmetric situation.
In addition, by analogy with [38, Conjecture 1.1], we expect that up to a suitable completion and localization,
the mode algebra of WN=2
∞
is isomorphic to the affine super Yangian of bsln+1|n [99, 100], and the Zhu algebra
of WN=2
∞
is isomorphic to the finite super Yangian of sln+1|n. This would imply that there are surjective
maps from this super affine Yangian to the mode algebras of all the vertex algebras Cψ
N =2(n, r|s). This is
analogous to the realization of finite W-superalgebras as truncations of super Yangians [21].
Three major topics of current interest are conformal embeddings [2, 6, 5], the reduction in stages conjecture
[61, 62], and the iterated reduction conjecture [26]. It is work in progress to prove the reduction in stages
and iterated reduction conjecture for Wk
N=1(sln+1+r|n+s, fn+1|n), as well as to find conformal embeddings
of W-algebras into W-superalgebras.
1.5. Organization. In Sections 2, 3, and 4, we recall some basic facts about vertex algebras, SUSY vertex
algebras, and the N = 2 superconformal algebra Virc
N =2 and its representation theory. In Section 5, we
discuss basic properties of Wk(sln+1|n), and in Section 6 we introduce the N = 2 Y -algebras Cψ
N =2(n, r|s),
which generalize Wk(sln+1|n). In Section 7, we construct the universal 2-parameter vertex algebra WN =2
∞
of
type (1.13). In Section 8, we prove that the N = 2 Y -algebras all arise as 1-parameter quotients of WN =2
∞
and we give their explicit truncation curves. In Section 9, we prove Theorem 1.5, and in Section 10 we study
the module category of the rational vertex superalgebras appearing in Theorem 1.6. Finally, we include
three appendices. In Appendix A, we prove that the generators of WN =2
∞
can be organized into primary
diamonds, proving the original conjecture of Candu and Gaberdiel [22]. Finally, in Appendices B and C we
extend the results of [39, 40] on cosets of affine vertex algebras inside larger structures, to cosets of affine
vertex superalgebras.

2.2. SUSY vertex algebras. Recall that an odd endomorphism D on a vertex algebra V is called an odd
derivation if it satisfies
(2.6)
[D, a(j)] = (Da)(j)
for any a ∈V and j ∈Z. When V has n supercommuting odd derivations D1, . . . , Dn satisfying D2
i = ∂, we
say that V has n supersymmetries. We call a vertex algebra equipped with n supersymmetries an N = n
supersymmetric (SUSY) vertex algebra. In particular, when N = 1, we often omit N = 1 and refer to it
simply as a SUSY vertex algebra.
Example 2.1. Let g be a finite basic simple Lie superalgebra with a supersymmetric invariant bilinear form
( | ) normalized by (θ|θ) = 2 for the even highest root θ of g. Let ¯g be the parity reversed superspace of g. The
SUSY affine vertex algebra V k
N=1(g) is freely generated by a basis {ai}i∈I of g and {¯ai}i∈I, where the OPE
relations between the generators are
(2.7)
¯ai(z)¯aj(w) ∼(k + h∨)(ai|aj)(z −w)−1,
¯ai(z)aj(w) ∼(−1)p(ai)p(aj)[ai, aj](w)(z −w)−1 ,
ai(z)aj(w) ∼(−1)p(ai)p(aj)
[ai, aj](w)(z −w)−1 + (k + h∨) (ai|aj)(z −w)−2 
.
This is a SUSY vertex algebra with a supersymmetry D defined by D(¯a) = a for any a ∈g. Note that
V k
N=1(g) ∼= V k(g) ⊗F(g), where F(g) is the free field vertex subalgebra of V k
N =1(g) generated by ¯g.
2.3. N = 1 superconformal algebras. In this section, we recall the notion of N = 1 and N = 2 super-
conformality introduced in [66]. Recall that a vertex algebra V is called conformal if it has an even element
L such that
L(z)L(w) ∼∂L(w)(z −w)−1 + 2L(w)(z −w)−2 + c
2(z −w)−4,
L(0) = ∂,
and V is diagonalizable with the L(1)-action, where the eigenvalues are bounded below. In this case, L is
called a conformal vector of central charge c ∈C. Generalizing the notion, one can define superconformal
vectors inside N = 1 or 2 SUSY vertex algebra.
Consider a SUSY vertex algebra V with a supersymmetry D. We say V is (N = 1) superconformal if it
has an odd element G, such that (i) L := 1
2DG is a conformal vector of central charge c, (ii) G(0) = D, and
(iii) L and G generate the Neveu-Schwarz vertex algebra Virc
N =1 of central charge c, that is,
(2.8)
L(z)G(w) ∼∂G(w)(z −w)−1 + 3
2G(w)(z −w)−2,
G(z)G(w) ∼2L(w)(z −w)−1 + 2
3c (z −w)−3.
We call such G an (N = 1) superconformal vector of central charge c. Conversely, in a conformal vertex
algebra, one can find a supersymmetry using an odd G satisfying (2.8).
Lemma 2.2. Let V be a conformal vertex algebra with a conformal vector L and an odd element G satisfying
(2.8). Then V is N = 1 superconformal with a superconformal vector G and D = G(0).
Proof. It is enough to show that D := G(0) is an odd endomorphism satisfying D2 = ∂. By the second
equality in (2.8), we have [D, D] = [G(0), G(0)] = (G(0)G)(0) = (2L)(0) = 2∂, which proves the desired
statement.
□
2.4. Free field algebras. A free field algebra is a vertex superalgebra V with weight grading
V =

with strong generators {Xi|i ∈I} satisfying OPE relations
Xi(z)Xj(w) ∼ai,j(z −w)−∆(Xi)−∆(Xj),
ai,j ∈C,
ai,j = 0 if ∆(Xi) + ∆(Xj) ̸∈Z.
Note V is not assumed to have a conformal structure. In [41], the first and third authors introduced the
following families of free field algebras.
(1) Even algebras of orthogonal type Oev(n, k) for n ≥1 and even k ≥2. When k = 2, Oev(n, 2) is just
the rank n Heisenberg algebra H(n).
(2) Odd algebras of orthogonal type Oodd(n, k) for n ≥1 and odd k ≥1. When k = 1, Oodd(n, 1) is
just the rank n free fermion algebra F(n).
7


## Page 8


(3) Even algebras of symplectic type Sev(n, k) for n ≥1 and odd k ≥1. When k = 1, Sodd(n, 1) is just
the rank n βγ-system S(n).
(4) Odd algebras of symplectic type Sodd(n, k) for n ≥1 and even k ≥2. When k = 2, Sev(n, 2) is just
the rank n symplectic fermion algebra A(n).
We refer the reader to [41] for the construction and key properties of these algebras. The main application
of them in this paper is that if g is a simple Lie superalgebra with a nondegenerate, supersymmetric bilinear
form, Wk(g, F) admits a large level limit Wfree(g, F) which is a tensor product of free field algebras of these
kinds. This is useful for deducing the generating type of orbifolds and affine cosets of Wk(g, F).

field, this is the same as the usual notion of simplicity. If I ⊆R is an ideal, I is a subset of V[0] ∼= R, and
we denote by I · V the VOA generated by I. Then VI = V/(I · V) is a VOA over R/I. Even if V is simple
as a vertex algebra over R, VI need not be simple as a vertex algebra over R/I.
Suppose that R is the coordinate ring O(X) of a variety X, and that V is simple. Let I ⊆R be an ideal
such that VI is not simple, i.e., VI has maximal proper graded ideal I with I[0] = {0}. Then the quotient
VI = VI/I
is a simple VOA over R/I. Letting Y ⊆X be the closed subvariety corresponding to I, we can regard
VI as a simple VOA over Y . For ideals I1, I2 with this property, we have the corresponding simple VOAs
VI1 = VI1/I1 and VI2 = VI2/I2 over R/I1 and R/I2, respectively. Let Y1, Y2 ⊆X be the closed subvarieties

from the description in [7], and it was checked for n = 2 in [4]. In Section 9, we will interpret this conjecture
as a limiting case of Ito’s conjecture.
6. N = 2 hook-type W-algebras and Y -algebras
In this section we generalize considerations of the previous one. First we introduce a family that generalizes
N = 2 principal W-superalgebras called N = 2 hook-type W-superalgebra; these are associated to even N = 2
hook-type nilpotent element
F = Fn+1,1r|n,1s ∈sln+r+1|n+s,
which is principal in n+1|n block and trivial in r|s block. Although we call these algebras N = 2 hook-type,
these do not have the N = 2 structure. In addition to the strong generators arising from the N = 2 principal
block as in Section 5, the trivial block gives rise an affine subVOA, and certain modules over it. We regard
this family as the N = 2 analogue of the hook-type algebras in type A.
The N = 2 Y -algebras will be defined as the cosets of these W-superalgebras by the affine subVOA
arising from the trivial block. They are a generalization of Wk(sln+1|n) which is the case r = s = 0, and
they satisfy analogous properties to Propositions 5.1, 5.4, 5.5; see Theorem 6.1. There is one more family
of such algebras which do not arise as W-algebras or their cosets, but are the N = 2 analogues of the GKO
cosets (1.6); see Theorem 6.2. The N = 2 Y -algebras are generally not simple, but they are simple in the
cases s = 0 or r = 0. We will introduce a different notation (6.25) for these two families, and some of our
results such as Proposition 6.4 and Theorem 9.2, will only be proven in these cases.
6.1. N = 2 hook-type W-algebras. Let g = sln+r+1|n+s which has a natural embedding of sln+1|n. For a
concrete picture, consider g as composed of block matrices with the index set I = {1, 2, . . . , 2n + r + s + 1}
as in Figure 2. In particular, we set the parity of the matrix by p(eij) = ¯ı + ¯ȷ (mod 2), where ¯ı is the parity
of the index i ∈I defined as follows:
(1) The first n + 1|n block is as in Section 5, i.e., the first 2n + 1 indices have parity ¯ı = i + 1 (mod 2).
(2) The second diagonal r|s block forms glr|s, where we let the first r are even, and the last s are odd.
To be explicit, the parity of the index is 2n + 1 + l = |l|, where
(6.1)

In [39], the first and third authors introduced a method to study cosets of affine vertex algebras V k(g)
inside larger vertex algebras Ak when g is a reductive Lie algebra. Under fairly general hypotheses that hold
when Ak is any W-(super)algebra, the coset has the same generating type as its large level limit, which is
the G-orbifold of a free field algebra, where G is the adjoint group of g. By [41, Theorem 5.3], the analogous

By [40, Theorem 4.1], if g is a simple Lie superalgebra, F ∈g is a nilpotent element, and a ⊆g♮is a
reductive Lie subalgebra, the coset of Wk(g, F) by the affine subVOA corresponding to a is strongly finitely
generated. We expect that the strong generating sets (6.15) and (6.22) for Cψ
N =2(n, r|s) are not minimal, and
that these vertex algebras are strongly finitely generated. The ideal of relations among the generators of the
invariant ring (6.17) are given by Sergeev’s second fundamental theorem of invariant theory for the standard
representation of GLr|s [95, Theorem 2.2]. These relations are certain linear combinations of products of
determinants with appropriate signs, and it is straightforward to check that the relation of minimal conformal
weight has even parity and conformal weight given by
(6.23)
ζ(n, r|s) :=
(

r ≥s,
(1 + r)(1 + n + 2s + ns + rs),
r < s.

Cψ
N=2(n, r|s); in other words, in the corresponding normally ordered relation, the coefficient of ωζ(n,r|s),⊥
is nonzero. If this is the case, it is easy to construct higher decoupling relations inductively for all fields
{ωi,⊥, ωi,±, ωi,⊤| i ≥ζ(n, r|s)}. This motivates the following.

we consider these cases to be most important and we introduce the notation

Proposition 6.4. Conjecture 6.3 is true for the families Dψ
N =2(n, s) and Eψ
N =2(n, r).
Proof. In these cases, Sergeev’s first and second fundamental theorems of invariant theory for the standard
representation of GLr|s reduces to Weyl’s first and second fundamental theorems for the standard repre-
sentation of the general linear group [101]. The relations are now ordinary determinants with appropriate
signs, and the conformal weight of the relation of minimal weight is given by (6.23). As usual, there is a
normally ordered relation among the generators and their derivatives of Eψ
N =2(n, r) and Dψ
N =2(n, s) whose
leading term is this classical relation. The lower order terms are quantum corrections, and one can show
that the lowest weight relation is a decoupling relation for ωζ(n,r|0),⊥or ωζ(n,0,s+1),⊥, respectively, by finding
a recursive formula for the coefficient of this term. This argument was introduced by the third authors in a
series of papers [78, 79, 80], and was used extensively in [40], so the details are omitted.
□

## Page 23


The outline of this section is as follows.
(1) We begin with the base case computation. Here, we write down the most general OPEs in S≤9
compatible with the N = 2 symmetry, and we impose vertex algebra relations (2.1-2.4) along with
Jacobi identities J≤9 to uniquely determine S≤7 in terms of two parameters c and λ. Furthermore,
we are able to find two commuting W∞(c±, λ±) subVOAs, see Proposition 7.8.
(2) Next, we compute an infinite set of structure constants in Proposition 7.12 and describe an induction
procedure which expresses all OPEs in Sn+1 in terms of S≤n thanks to Jacobi relations in Jn+2 (7.14).
(3) Lastly, we exhibit a family of vertex algebras with known characters to prove free generation of
WN=2
∞
. Specifically, we use the family of W-superalgebras Wk(sln+1|n), see Proposition 7.19.
Before we begin, we would like to comment on a closely related work of Candu and Gaberdiel [22], where
authors have done a similar computation to the following subsection under the assumption that all diamonds
W N are Virc
N=2 primary for N ≥2. There, the authors have conjectured the existence and uniqueness of
WN=2
∞
as a 2-parameter vertex algebra. In this section and Appendix A, we prove their conjecture.
7.2. Base case computation. In the ansatz (7.8) we have already imposed the Heisenberg and confor-
mal weight gradation arising from the zero mode H(0) of the Heisenberg generator, and the first mode
L(1) of the Virasoro generator, together with relations (7.9); equivalently, the Jacobi identities of the form
J1,r(L, W 2, W 2) and J0,r(H, W 2, W 2) vanish. We proceed to impose the full superconformal symmetry con-
straints on OPEs W 2(z)W 2(w); equivalently, we impose the Jacobi identities of the form J(W 1, W 2, W 2).
Remarkably, the imposition of N = 2 constraints J(W 1, W 2, W 2) expresses OPEs in W 2(z)W 2(w) in terms
of the scaling parameter ω and two parameters c and λ, defined in the following.

Proposition 7.8. The Jacobi relations in J9 express all OPEs in D7 in terms of two parameters, the central

given in Propositions 7.2 and 7.6.

Recall that if A is an N = 2 supersymmetric vertex algebra and V embeds in A (not necessarily confor-
mally), A is called an N = 2 supersymmetric extension of V [97]. We call A a minimal N = 2 supersymmetric
extension if no proper N = 2-superconformal subalgebra of A is an N = 2 supersymmetric extension of V.

has the same graded character as W−1(sln+1|n). According to the conjecture of [4], this is the correct way
to take the large level limit of Cℓ(n) so that Ito’s conjecture holds at the critical level.
10. Level rank duality via mirror equivalence
In this section, we study the representation theory of strongly rational vertex superalgebras, and we apply
our results to the W-algebras Wk(sln+1|n) in Theorem 9.5.
49


## Page 50


10.1. Mirror equivalence for vertex superalgebras. Let V be a simple vertex superalgebra with the
decomposition V = V0 ⊕V1 according to the parity. While for vertex algebras one usually is only interested
in the category of local modules, in the super case one often also likes to consider so-called Ramond-twisted
modules. Both categories together are best described via the even subalgebra V0 of V .
Let V0 be a vertex tensor category of V0-modules that contains V1 as an object. Let V be the supercategory
of local and also Ramond twisted modules of V . This category can be studied using the theory of vertex
superalgebra extensions [36]. In particular, V can be identified with a commutative superalgebra in V0 [34].
Let SV0 = V0 ⊠sVect be the supercategory associated to V0. The underlying category C of a supercategory
C has the same objects as C, but morphisms are required to be even. Then V can also be identified with a
commutative algebra in SV0 whose underlying object is (V0, V1) that is the object whose even part is V0 and
whose odd part is V1. It comes with a multiplication morphism m : V ⊠V →V . The category V is then
precisely the underlying category of modules for the commutative algebra V that lie in SV0. Then there is
an induction functor from SV0 to V
(10.1)
Ind : SV0 →V,
X 7→(V ⊠X, m ⊠idX),
f 7→IdV ⊠f,
This functor is monoidal and by composing it with the embedding of V0 in SV0, (10.1) lifts to a functor
(10.2)
IndV : V0 →V,
X 7→(V ⊠(X, 0), m ⊠id(X,0)),
f 7→IdV ⊠(f, 0),
which we also call induction. According to [27, Theorem A.1], the induction (10.2) is an equivalence as
tensor categories.
Proposition 10.1. [36, Prop. 4.22] Under the above set-up, that is in particular V = V0 ⊕V1 is a vertex
superalgebra with even and odd part V0 and V1 and V is an object in a vertex tensor category V0 of V0-
modules, then V1 is a simple current with V1 ⊠V1 ∼= V0 and for X a simple object in V with X = X0 ⊕X1
its decomposition into even and odd part, then X0 is simple and X1 ∼= V1 ⊠X0.
In the case of this proposition, we call X a local (or a Neveu-Schwarz) module if it is a module for the
vertex superalgebra V , and otherwise we call it a (Ramond) twisted module.
Let W be another simple vertex superalgebra with even subalgebra W0 and vertex tensor category W0
of W0-modules. Let n : W ⊠W →W denote the multiplication of the corresponding superalgebra object
in W0. Let H : V0 →W0 be a functor such that H(V ) ∼= W. If H is a functor of vertex tensor categories,
then the multiplication on V induces one on H(V ), that is, (H(V ), H(m)) is a commutative superalgebra in
W0. The resulting algebra is simple if H is fully faithful. By [34, Remark 3.11], the superalgebra structure
is unique up to isomorphism and hence (H(V ), H(m)) ∼= (W, n). It follows that if H is an equivalence, then
it induces an equivalence V ∼= W of the corresponding objects in V with the functor IndW ◦H ◦(IndV )−1.
This means that equivalences of categories V0 and W0 of even subalgebras can be lifted to equivalences of
categories V and W for the vertex superalgebras that include the Ramond twisted modules. We apply this
observation to the mirror equivalence.
The following theorem is called mirror equivalence for vertex algebras. A first version was proven for
unitary vertex algebras [77] and was generalized in [37, 85].
Theorem 10.2. Let U and V be simple self-contragredient vertex operator algebras such that:
• There is an injective conformal vertex algebra homomorphism ιA : U ⊗V →A, where A is a simple
conformal vertex algebra.
• A is a U ⊗V -module, and
A =

where the U i are distinct simple U-modules and the V i are semisimple V -modules. Let 0 ∈I denote
the index such that U 0 = U.
• The vertex subalgebras U and V form a dual pair in A in the sense that V 0 = V and dim HomV (V, V i) =
δi,0 for all i ∈I.
• The U-modules U i for i ∈I are objects of a locally-finite semisimple braided ribbon tensor category
U of U-modules that is closed under contragredients.
• The V -modules V i for i ∈I are objects of a braided tensor category V of grading- restricted gener-
alized V -modules which is closed under submodules, quotients, and contragredients.
50


## Page 51


Let UA (respectively, VA) denote the category of U-modules (respectively, V -modules) whose objects are finite
direct sums of the U i (respectively, of the V i) for i ∈I. Then:
(1) The category UA of U-modules is a tensor subcategory of U.
(2) The category VA of V -modules is a semisimple ribbon tensor subcategory of V with distinct simple
objects {V i|i ∈I}. In particular, VA is rigid.
(3) There is a braid-reversed tensor equivalence τ : UA →VA such that τ(Ui) ∼= (V i)′ for i ∈I, where
(V i)′ is the contragredient dual of V i.
This theorem generalizes to A being a vertex superalgebra [85]. The purpose of this section is to adapt
this theorem to the case where U and V are allowed to be vertex superalgebras.
Before doing so, we note that in the special case that U, V are strongly rational and that A is holomorphic,
that is, every module of A is isomorphic to a direct sum of copies of A, then the categories UA, VA in Theorem
10.2 are in fact U ∼= UA and V ∼= VA. The reason is that their Frobenius-Perron dimensions coincide, since
the category of modules of the vertex algebra A is the category of local modules of the corresponding
commutative algebra object A [67, 36] and since in this setting the following relation holds [51, Cor.3.32]

As a consequence of (10.8) and (10.9) we get that C(n, m) is a subalgebra of E(nm + n + m) and its
commutant is a conformal extension of Ln+1(slm) ⊗Lm+1(sln) times a rank two Heisenberg vertex algebra.
We now want to determine this commutant more explicitly and then derive the branching rules, that is explain
how E(nm+n+m) decomposes into modules for the tensor product of these two commuting subalgebras and
in particular via applying the mirror equivalence of the previous section we will then get the classification
of simple modules and simple Ramond twisted modules of C(n, m) as well as their fusion rules.

10.4. Results at admissible levels. Recall that a level ℓof sln is called admissible if ℓ= −n + u
v with
u, v coprime positive integers and u ≥n. The representation theory of the simple affine vertex algebra
Lℓ(sln) at a non-integral admissible level is expected to be still quite nice. In general it is known that the
category of Lℓ(sln)-modules that lie in the category O is semisimple [11] and that the category of Lℓ(sln)-
modules that are integrable as sln-modules (also called the Kazhdan-Lusztig category or the category of
ordinary modules) is a ribbon tensor category [33, 23].
If the admissible level k is integral then every
Lℓ(sln)module is integrable as an sln-module. At non-integral admissible levels there are continuous families
of modules that are not integrable as a sln-module and not even in the category O. These modules have
infinite dimensional conformal weight spaces and usually the conformal weight is not lower-bounded, see
e.g. [70] and see [15] for descriptions of categories appearing. Hence the category of the dual W-algebra
Wk(sln|n−1) ∼= Com(Lℓ+1(gln−1), Lℓ(sln) ⊗E(n −1)) also have continuous families of modules. Note that
here k = −1 + v
u.
As not much is known beyond sl2, we now restrict to this case. The category of weight modules of Lℓ(sl2)
at admissible level has been studied and there is a full classification [15]. This category is a vertex tensor
category [24], it is ribbon [45, 88], fusion rules are determined [25, 88] and the logarithmic Verlinde formula of
[47] holds [25]. In other words, this category is under great control. The Kazama-Suzuki duality to Wk(sl2|1)
is such that all these results transport to the category of weight modules of Wk(sl2|1) for k = −1 + v
u and
this has been worked out in great detail by many [88, 44, 93]. In particular the category of weight modules of
Wk(sl2|1) for k = −1 + v
u for u, v coprime positive integers and v ≥2 is a locally finite ribbon supercategory,
modules are classified and fusion rules are known [88].
We expect that similar results hold for higher rank, but likely only for certain good admissible levels. This
is exemplified in the case of Lℓ(sl3) at the admissible level ℓ= −3 + u
v with u, v coprime positive integers
and u ≥3. In this case the representation theory is much nicer for v = 2 then for v > 2, see [3, 48, 52, 71].
Beyond the cases of sl2 and sl3 there are only few results [70], and it is a major goal of the community to
improve the situation.
A. Appendix
A.1. Singular vectors and extensions. Here we recall some notions of Virc
N =2-modules. Let C be the
category of ordinary real weight Virc
N=2-modules, i.e., an object M of C carries an action of Virc
N =2 so that:
(1) M admits a grading by conformal weights M = L
n∈R Mn, with Mn = 0 for n << 0,
(2) weight spaces Mn are finite-dimensional,
(3) weight spaces Mn decompose into Heisenberg weight spaces, on which H(0) acts semisimply.
Let V (c, h, j) be the Verma module whose lowest conformal weight is h and the lowest conformal weight
space has Heisenberg weight j, and L(c, h, j) be its simple quotient.
Lemma A.1. For irrational c, h ∈1

Proof. Let N, M in S and consider a short exact sequence
0 →N →R →M →0.
Let hN, hM be the conformal weight of their top levels and jN, jM be their Heisenberg weights.
If hM < hN then the top level of R coincides with the top level of M. Since M is generated by the top
level it follows that either the short exact sequence splits or R is also generated by the top level. Assume
that the short exact sequence is non-split. By the universal property of Verma modules, R needs to be a
homomorphic image of V (c, hM, jM). This is impossible if M ∼= V (c, hM, jM). If M ∼= L(c, 0, 0) then by the
previous corollary the only possibilities would be N ∼= V (c, 1
2, ±1) but these are not in S.
The contragredient dual functor is covariant and strong exact (maps non-split short exact sequences to
non-split short exact sequences). The contragredient dual of V (c, h, j) is V (c, h, −j) and the one of L(c, 0, 0)
is L(c, 0, 0) itself. That is, S is closed under the contragredient dual, and hence the corollary is also true for
hM > hN.
If hM = hN, then necessarily N ∼= M and the top level of R is 2-dimensional and a module for the
commutative algebra CL(1) ⊕CH(0). As we assume that L0 and H0 act semisimply, the top level is a direct
sum of two simple modules for this zero-mode subalgebra. By the universal property of Verma modules, R is
thus a homomorphic image of V (c, hM, jM)⊕V (c, hM, jM). In particular if M is itself a Verma module then
R ∼= V (c, hM, jM) ⊕V (c, hM, jM) and if M ∼= L(c, 0, 0) then by the previous corollary the only possibility is
R ∼= L(c, 0, 0) ⊕L(c, 0, 0).
□

B. Properties of strong generators
The purpose of this section is to generalize some results of [40, Section 3] to Lie superalgebras, especially
[40, Lemma 3.1].
Let g be a finite-dimensional basic, classical Lie superalgebra, or gln|n.
In particular
g admits a non-degenerate, invariant, consistent, supersymmetric bilinear form B. Let g = g0 ⊕g1 be the
decomposition into its even and odd parts. Let a be a direct summand of g. Then a0 is a direct sum of abelian
and simple Lie algebras. If a0 has a simple summand then we choose a simple summand and normalize B
on a such that long roots of this simple summand have norm two, otherwise a ∈{gl1, gl1|1, psl1|1} and we
normalize B on a such that the odd positive root has norm one for a ∈{gl1|1, psl1|1} (for a = gl1 we can

call it the normalized bilinear form.

Definition B.1. Let V be a vertex operator superalgebra and V -mod a category of V -modules.
(1) An object M in V -mod is called almost semisimple if it is lower-bounded by conformal weight and if
every proper submodule N intersects the top level of M nontrivially.
(2) V -mod is called almost semisimple if every object is a direct sum of almost semisimple objects.

0 of eigenvalue −k,
and x(ω) = 0 if k > m.
It follows that U(g+)ω is a finite-dimensional vector space graded by conformal weight. In particular, the
subspace M ⊆U(g+)ω of minimal conformal weight is finite-dimensional. Hence it is a finite-dimensional
g-module. Moreover, U(g+) acts trivially on M. Since g is indecomposable and Lg is the Sugawara vector

k /∈K.

So far, we have reproven statements of [39, Section 6] in the setting where g is a simple basic classical Lie
superalgebra or g = glr|s. The proofs of the following statements are now the same as the corresponding
statements in [39, Section 6]. From now on g is an arbitrary element in S, and B is nondegenerate.
Corollary C.6. (cf. [39, Corollary 6.8]) Suppose that g is in S, B is nondegenerate and the restriction of B
to each indecomposable direct summand gi ⊆g is the normalized bilinear form on gi. Then C is a deformable
family over FK and Ck = Com(V k(g, B), Ak) for all k such that k + h∨
i /∈Q, for all i. Here h∨
i is the dual
Coxeter number of the gi.

[39, Remark 6.9]) If the restriction of B to gi is a nonzero constant λi times the
normalized bilinear form on gi, the above statement must be modified as follows: C is a deformable family
over FK and Ck = Com(V k(g, B), Ak) for all k such that kλi + h∨
i /∈Q, for all i.
Finally, we give a precise description of the limit C∞= limκ→∞C.
Theorem C.8. (cf. [39, Theorem 6.10]) Let g, B, and A be as above. Then we have a vertex algebra
isomorphism
lim
κ→∞C ∼= ˜
AG.
